\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}_{\sigma^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}}(i^{\star})>0\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-n^{3\varepsilon^{3}}).

Due to Lemma
8.31
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{\sigma^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}(i^{\star})>0\mathclose{]}\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{\sigma^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}(i^{\star})-Z_{\sigma^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}(\sigma^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}})>\delta^{2}\xi_{1}(\sigma^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}})\mathclose{]}\leq\sum_{0\leq i\leq i^{\star}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{i}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}(i^{\star})-Z_{i}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}>\delta^{2}\xi_{1}\mathclose{]}.

Thus, for
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
, it suffices to obtain

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{i}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}(i^{\star})-Z_{i}^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}>\delta^{2}\xi_{1}\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-n^{4\varepsilon^{3}}).

We show that this bound is a consequence of Freedman’s inequality for supermartingales.

Let us turn to the details.
Lemma
9.15
shows that
Z^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}_{i}(i),Z^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}_{i}(i+1),\ldots
is a supermartingale, while Lemma
9.17
provides the bound
\lvert\Delta Z^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}_{i}(j)\rvert\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\lparen\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{e}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{b},I}\rparen/(n\hat{p}^{\rho_{\mathcal{F}}})
for all
j\geq i
and Lemma
9.18
provides the bound
\sum_{j\geq i}\mathbb{E}_{j}\mathopen{[}\lvert\Delta Z^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}_{i}(j)\rvert\mathclose{]}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{e}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{b},I}
.
Hence, we may apply Lemma
8.20
such that using Lemma
9.9
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{i}\mathopen{[}Z^{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\phantom{\displaystyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\displaystyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\textstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\textstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}{\phantom{\scriptscriptstyle-}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\smash{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle-$\cr\rotatebox[origin={c}]{45.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\cr}}}}}}}_{i}(i^{\star})>0\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathcal{X}}\exp\Bigg\lparen-\frac{\delta^{4}\xi_{1}^{2}}{2n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\frac{\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{e}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{b},I}}{n\hat{p}^{\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}}(\xi_{1}+n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{e}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{b},I})}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg\lparen-\frac{\delta^{4}\xi_{1}^{2}n\hat{p}^{\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}}{4n^{2\varepsilon^{3}}\lparen\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{e}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{b},I}\rparen^{2}}\bigg\rparen\leq\exp\bigg\lparen-\frac{\delta^{5}n^{2\varepsilon^{2}}}{4n^{2\varepsilon^{3}}}\bigg\rparen\leq\exp(-n^{4\varepsilon^{3}}),

which completes the proof.
∎

10.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

In this section, we combine Lemma
7.20
with Lemma
8.32
and Lemma
9.20
to conclude that typically, we have
i^{\star}<\tau^{\star}
, see Lemma
10.1
, which in turn yields a proof for Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma 10.1
.

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau^{\star}\leq i^{\star}\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-\log n)^{4/3})
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
7.20
, Lemma
8.32
and Lemma
9.20
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau^{\star}\leq i^{\star}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\tau\in\mathopen{\{}\tau_{\mathcal{H}^{*}},\tau_{\mathscr{B}},\tau_{\mathscr{B}^{\prime}},\tau_{\mathfrak{C}},\tau_{\mathfrak{B}}\mathclose{\}}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau\leq\tau^{\star}\wedge i^{\star}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lparen\sum_{\tau\in\mathopen{\{}\tau_{\mathcal{H}^{*}},\tau_{\mathscr{B}},\tau_{\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}\mathclose{\}}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau\leq\tilde{\tau}^{\star}\wedge i^{\star}\mathclose{]}\Big\rparen+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau_{\mathfrak{C}}\leq\tilde{\tau}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{\star}\wedge i^{\star}\mathclose{]}+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau_{\mathfrak{B}}\leq\tau^{\star}\wedge i^{\star}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq 5\exp(-(\log n)^{3/2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau^{\star}\mathclose{\}}
,
i:=i^{\star}
and
\vartheta^{\star}:=\hat{p}
.
By Lemma
10.1
, it suffices to show that if
\mathcal{X}
occurs, then
\mathcal{H}
is
(4m,n^{\varepsilon})
-bounded,
\mathcal{F}
-populated,
k^{\prime}
-populated for all
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
and has
n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon}/k!
edges.

Due to
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau_{\mathscr{B}}\wedge\tau_{\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}\mathclose{\}}
, for all strictly balanced
k
-templates
(\mathcal{A},I)
with
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert\leq 1/\varepsilon^{4}
and all
\psi\colon I\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{H}}
, Lemma
7.14
yields

\Phi_{\mathcal{A},I}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}(1+\log n)^{\alpha_{\mathcal{A},I}}\max\mathopen{\{}1,\hat{\varphi}_{\mathcal{A},I}\mathclose{\}}\leq n^{\varepsilon}\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert}(\vartheta^{\star})^{\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{A}[I]\rvert}\mathclose{\}}

Thus,
H
is
(4m,n^{\varepsilon})
-bounded if
\mathcal{X}
occurs.

Furthermore, due to
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau_{\mathscr{F}}\mathclose{\}}
, for all
e\in\mathcal{H}
, Lemma
7.18
entails

d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)\geq_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\,\hat{\varphi}_{\mathcal{F},f}}{2\aut(\mathcal{F})}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\,n^{\varepsilon(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}}{2\aut(\mathcal{F})}\geq n^{\varepsilon^{2}},

which shows that
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{F}
-populated if
\mathcal{X}
occurs.

Let
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
and let
(\mathcal{A},I)
denote a
k
-template with
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert=k
,
\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert=1
and
\lvert I\rvert=k^{\prime}
.
Fix a
k^{\prime}
-set
U\subseteq V_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\psi\colon I\hookrightarrow U
.
We have
\rho_{\mathcal{A},I}\leq\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
, so for all
j\leq i
, Lemma
7.8
implies

\hat{\varphi}_{\mathcal{A},I}(j)\geq n^{k-k^{\prime}}\hat{p}^{\rho_{\mathcal{F}}(k-k^{\prime})}=n^{\varepsilon\rho_{\mathcal{F}}(k-k^{\prime})}\geq n^{\varepsilon^{2}}>\zeta^{-\delta^{1/2}}

and hence
i^{\star}<i_{\mathcal{A},I}^{\delta^{1/2}}
.
Thus, due to
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau_{\mathscr{B}}\mathclose{\}}
, we obtain

d_{\mathcal{H}}(U)=\frac{\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}}{(k-k^{\prime})!}\geq_{\mathcal{X}}\varepsilon\hat{\varphi}_{\mathcal{A},I}\geq n^{\varepsilon^{2}},

which shows that
\mathcal{H}
is
k^{\prime}
-populated if
\mathcal{X}
-occurs.

Finally, since
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau_{\emptyset}\mathclose{\}}
, Lemma
7.7
yields
H=_{\mathcal{X}}\vartheta^{\star}n^{k}/k!=n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon}/k!
.
∎

11.
The sparse setting

The first part of our argumentation is now complete and as mentioned in Section
2
, we now focus on the second part.
We first describe the setting for this section and subsequent sections and remark that from now on, we redefine some symbols that appeared in the first part.
Let
k\geq 2
and fix a
k
-graph
\mathcal{F}
on
m
vertices with
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\geq 2
and
k
-density
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
that is not a matching such that
(\mathcal{F},f)
is strictly balanced for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
.
Suppose that
0<\varepsilon<1
is sufficiently small in terms of
1/m
and that
n
is sufficiently large in terms of
1/\varepsilon
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{H}(0)
is a
k
-graph on
n
vertices with
n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{4}}\leq\lvert\mathcal{H}(0)\rvert\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{4}}
that is
(4m,n^{\varepsilon^{4}})
-bounded
1
1

1

Note that for
\mathcal{F}
, besides strictly
k
-balanced
k
-graphs, this setup also allows
k
-graphs as in Theorem
1.7
.
We choose this slightly more general setting as this makes many of the results we present available for a proof of Theorem
1.7
while only requiring very minor adaptations.

.

For the second part, that is for the proof of Theorems
1.5
and
1.7
, one key idea is the identification of substructures in
\mathcal{H}(0)
whose existence enforces the existence of edges that are no longer contained in a copy of
\mathcal{F}
with a substantial probability.
We show that there is a sufficiently large subset of these substructures whose members are far apart from each other and hence act, to a large extent, independently.
We employ a concentration inequality to verify that a substantial number of these substructures indeed give rise to edges that are no longer contained in a copy of
\mathcal{F}
and hence remain until the termination of the process.

On a very high level, similar ideas have also been utilized by Bohman, Frieze and Lubetzky for determining the number of remaining edges in the triangle-removal process (starting at
K_{n}
), see
[
6
, Section 6]
.
In our significantly more general setting however, we require additional insights concerning the distribution of copies of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{H}(0)
.
Notably, while in the special case where
\mathcal{F}
is a triangle, two distinct copies of
\mathcal{F}
that both contain an edge
e
cannot overlap outside
e
, such overlaps can exist in general.
However, since
(\mathcal{F},f)
is strictly balanced for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, if two copies of
\mathcal{F}
, both containing an edge
e
, overlap outside
e
, then their union forms a
k
-graph with
k
-density greater than
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
.
As a crucial step in our proof, we utilize this to show that certain substructures consisting of copies of
\mathcal{F}
barely exist in the sense that we obtain a strong upper bound on the number of such structures.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
12
, we prove several structural results which are important for the following parts.
This includes properties of the aforementioned substructures that yield the edges that still remain at the end of the process.
In Section
13
, we obtain an upper bound on the number of remaining copies that holds well beyond the point where we would expect the process to terminate (this general idea is taken from
[
6
]
).
To this end, we again employ an approach that resembles the
differential equation method
or more specifically the
critical interval method
.

Combining the structural results from Section
12
and the upper bound on the number of edges at a very late time in the process obtained in Section
13
, we finally prove Theorem
1.5
in Section
14
.
As mentioned above, here the idea is to identify certain configurations that have to appear frequently before the process terminates and that with sufficiently large probability lead to edges that remain in the hypergraph until termination.
Compared to the (in spirit) similar argument in
[
6
, Section 6]
here the (involved) insights from Section
12
replace properties that are obvious in the triangle case.

For Theorem
1.7
, one may argue very similarly, however, the structures that in the end enforce the existence of edges that remain until termination are different.
In more detail, to obtain Theorem
1.7
, parts of the argumentation in Section
12
and the key structures considered in Section
14
need to be replaced but the results from Section
13
remain valid and the high level structure of the proof remains the same.
For completeness, we provide a full proof of Theorem
1.7
in Appendix
D
.

12.
Unions of strictly balanced hypergraphs

In this section, as preparation for the arguments in subsequent sections, we gather some lemmas that provide further insight into the distribution of the copies of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{H}
.
First, we state several lemmas concerning the densities of substructures obtained as unions of
k
-balanced
k
-graphs (see Lemmas
12.2
–
12.5
).
In particular, we are interested in structures that are in a sense cyclic, where formally for
\ell\geq 2
, we say that a sequence
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
of distinct
k
-graphs forms a
self-avoiding cyclic walk
if there exist distinct
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
such that
e_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
with indices taken modulo
\ell
.

From the
(4m,n^{\varepsilon^{4}})
-boundedness of
\mathcal{H}(0)
, we then deduce Lemma
12.6
where for all
k
-graphs
\mathcal{A}
that satisfy a suitable density property, we bound the number
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}
of injections
\varphi\colon V_{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{H}}
with
\varphi(e)\in\mathcal{H}(0)
for all
e\in\mathcal{A}
where we set
V_{\mathcal{H}}:=V_{\mathcal{H}(0)}
.

Using
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\geq 1/(k-1)
(see Lemma
12.7
), the aforementioned density observations allow us to apply Lemma
12.6
to then obtain Lemma
12.8
as an intermediate result and subsequently Lemma
12.9
which states that
\mathcal{H}(0)
contains only few cyclic structures formed by copies of
\mathcal{F}
.
This turns out to be a crucial observation concerning the structure of
\mathcal{H}(0)
that we require in two separate places in our argumentation (namely in the proofs of Lemma
13.11
and Lemma
14.2
).

As these objects frequently appear in our proofs, we generalize the notation
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}
as follows.
For a template
(\mathcal{A},I)
and
\psi\colon I\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{H}}
, we use
\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}^{\sim}
to denote the set of injections
\varphi\colon V_{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{H}}
with
\varphi|_{I}=\psi
and
\varphi(e)\in\mathcal{H}(0)
for all
e\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{A}[I]
and we set
\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}:=\lvert\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}^{\sim}\rvert
.
Additionally, we define
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\sim}:=\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}^{\sim}
where
\psi
denotes the unique function from
\emptyset
to
V_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Note that
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}=\lvert\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\sim}\rvert
.

The bounds on
\lvert\mathcal{H}(0)\rvert
and the numbers of embeddings of strictly balanced templates into
\mathcal{H}(0)
yield the following lemma.

Lemma 12.1
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{A},I)
is a strictly balanced
k
-template with
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert\leq 4m
and let
\psi\colon I\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then,
\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert-(\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{A}[I]\rvert)/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}\mathclose{\}}
.

Proof.

We have
\lvert\mathcal{H}(0)\rvert\leq n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+2\varepsilon^{4}}\cdot n^{k}/k!
, so since
\mathcal{H}(0)
is
(4m,n^{\varepsilon^{4}})
-bounded, we obtain

\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{4}}\cdot\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert}n^{(-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+2\varepsilon^{4})(\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{A}[I]\rvert)}\mathclose{\}}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert-(\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{A}[I]\rvert)/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}\mathclose{\}},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
is a sequence of
k
-balanced
k
-graphs with
k
-density at least
\rho
.
For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, let
\mathcal{S}_{i}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Suppose that for all
2\leq i\leq\ell
, we have
\mathcal{S}_{i-1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
and
J\subsetneq V_{\mathcal{S}}
with
\mathcal{S}[J]\neq\emptyset
.
Then,
\rho_{\mathcal{S},J}\geq\rho
.

Proof.

By rearranging the elements of
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
if necessary, we may assume that
\mathcal{A}_{1}[J]\neq\emptyset
.
For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, let

\begin{gathered}U:=V_{\mathcal{S}}\setminus J,\quad E:=\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}[J],\quad W_{i-1}:=V_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup V_{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}},\\
J_{i}:=(J\cup W_{i-1})\cap V_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},\quad U_{i}:=V_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\setminus J_{i},\quad E_{i}:=\mathcal{A}_{i}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{i}[J_{i}].\end{gathered}

Note that
U=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq\ell}U_{i}
and
U_{i}\cap U_{j}=\emptyset
for all
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
.
Hence,
\lvert U\rvert=\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\lvert U_{i}\rvert
.
Similarly, we have
E\supseteq\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq\ell}E_{i}
and
E_{i}\cap E_{j}=\emptyset
for all
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
and thus
\lvert E\rvert\geq\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\lvert E_{i}\rvert
.
This yields

\rho_{\mathcal{S},J}=\frac{\lvert E\rvert}{\lvert U\rvert}\geq\frac{\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\lvert E_{i}\rvert}{\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\lvert U_{i}\rvert}.

Let
e_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}[J]
and for
2\leq i\leq\ell
, let
e_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{S}_{i-1}
.
For all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, the extension
(\mathcal{A}_{i},e_{i})
is balanced and has density at least
\rho
, so due to
e_{i}\subseteq J_{i}
, we obtain

\lvert E_{i}\rvert=\rho_{\mathcal{A}_{i},e_{i}}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\rvert-k)-\rho_{\mathcal{A}_{i}[J_{i}],e_{i}}(\lvert J_{i}\rvert-k)\geq\rho_{\mathcal{A}_{i},e_{i}}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\rvert-k)-\rho_{\mathcal{A}_{i},e_{i}}(\lvert J_{i}\rvert-k)=\rho_{\mathcal{A}_{i},e_{i}}\lvert U_{i}\rvert\geq\rho\lvert U_{i}\rvert.

Hence, we obtain

\rho_{\mathcal{S},J}\geq\frac{\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\rho\lvert U_{i}\rvert}{\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\lvert U_{i}\rvert}=\rho,

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
is a sequence of
k
-balanced
k
-graphs with
k
-density at least
\rho
.
For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, let
\mathcal{S}_{i}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Suppose that for all
2\leq i\leq\ell
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{S}_{i-1}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
.
Then,
\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}\geq\rho
or
(\mathcal{S},\emptyset)
is strictly balanced.

Proof.

Suppose that
\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}<\rho
.
We show that then
(\mathcal{S},\emptyset)
is strictly balanced.
To this end, consider
(\mathcal{C},\emptyset)\subseteq(\mathcal{S},\emptyset)
with
V_{\mathcal{C}}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{C}\neq\mathcal{S}
.
It suffices to show that
\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset}<\rho_{\mathcal{S},\emptyset}
.

First, note that we may assume that
\mathcal{C}
is an induced subgraph of
\mathcal{S}
with non-empty edge set.
By Lemma
12.2
, we have
\rho_{\mathcal{S},V_{\mathcal{C}}}\geq\rho
and due to
\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}<\rho
furthermore
\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset}<\rho
.
Hence
\rho_{\mathcal{S},V_{\mathcal{C}}}>\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset}
.
Thus,

\rho_{\mathcal{S},\emptyset}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{S}[V_{\mathcal{C}}]\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{C}\rvert}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert}=\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{S},V_{\mathcal{C}}}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-\lvert V_{\mathcal{C}}\rvert)+\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset}\lvert V_{\mathcal{C}}\rvert}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert}>\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-\lvert V_{\mathcal{C}}\rvert)+\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset}\lvert V_{\mathcal{C}}\rvert}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert}=\rho_{\mathcal{C},\emptyset},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.4
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
is a sequence of strictly
k
-balanced
k
-graphs with
k
-density
\rho
that forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk such that no proper subsequence forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk.
Let
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
.
Then, there exists
e\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}>\rho
.

Proof.

First note that since no proper subsequence of
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}=\emptyset
for all
2\leq i\leq\ell-2
.
Furthermore if
\ell\geq 3
, then again since no proper subsequence of
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk, for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we have
\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i+1}\rvert=1
with indices taken modulo
\ell
(otherwise
\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i+1}
forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk).
Hence if
\ell\geq 3
, then
\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell-1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\rvert=\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{A}_{1}\rvert=1
.

For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, let
\mathcal{S}_{i}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
If
\ell\geq 3
, then, as a consequence of the above observations, due to
\lvert\mathcal{A}_{1}\rvert,\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\rvert\geq 3
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\neq\emptyset
as well as
\mathcal{A}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\neq\emptyset
.
If
\ell=2
, then
\mathcal{A}_{1}\not\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\not\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}
and hence
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\neq\emptyset
follow from the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are distinct strictly
k
-balanced
k
-graphs with the same
k
-density.
Let
e\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
.
As a consequence of Lemma
12.2
, we have
\rho_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1},e}\geq\rho
.
Hence,

\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}=\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1},e}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert-k)+\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\rvert}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert-k+\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert}\geq\frac{\rho(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert-k)+\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\rvert}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert-k+\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert}.

Thus, it suffices to show that
\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\rvert>\rho\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert
.
Due to
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\neq\emptyset
, the inequality holds if
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert=0
, so we may assume that
V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\neq\emptyset
.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk, there exist distinct
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
, so in particular, we have
e_{1}\subsetneq V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\cap V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\subsetneq V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}
.
The template
(\mathcal{A}_{\ell},e_{1})
is strictly balanced, so we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}\rvert

\displaystyle\geq\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{\ell}[V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\cap V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}]\rvert=\rho(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\rvert-k)-\rho_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}[V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\cap V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}],e_{1}}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\cap V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\rvert-k)

\displaystyle>\rho(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\rvert-k)-\rho(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\cap V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\rvert-k)=\rho\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{\ell}}\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell-1}}\rvert,

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
is a sequence of strictly
k
-balanced
k
-graphs with
k
-density
\rho
that forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk.
Let
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
.
Then, there exists
e\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}>\rho
.

Proof.

Consider a subsequence
\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{i_{\ell^{\prime}}}
of
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
that forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk such that no proper subsequence forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk.
Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{i_{\ell^{\prime}}}
.
By Lemma
12.4
, there exists
e\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},e}>\rho
and by Lemma
12.2
, if
V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\subsetneq V_{\mathcal{S}}
, then
\rho_{\mathcal{S},V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}}\geq\rho
.
This yields

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},e}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rvert-k)+\lvert\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\rvert}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k}\geq\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},e}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rvert-k)+\rho_{\mathcal{S},V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rvert)}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k}

\displaystyle>\frac{\rho(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rvert-k)+\rho(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rvert)}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k}=\rho,

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.6
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{A},I)
is a
k
-template with
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert\leq 1/\varepsilon
and
\rho_{\mathcal{A},J}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
for all
I\subseteq J\subsetneq V_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Let
\psi\colon I\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then,
\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{2}}
.

Proof.

We use induction on
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert
to show that

(12.1)

\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert)}.

Then, since
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert\leq 1/\varepsilon
, the statement follows.

If
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert=0
, then
\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}=1=\hat{\varphi}_{\mathcal{A},I}
.
Let
\ell\geq 1
and suppose that (
12.1
) holds if
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert\leq\ell-1
.
Suppose that
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert=\ell
.
Let
I\subseteq U\subseteq V_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{A}[U],I}
is maximal and subject to this, that
\lvert U\rvert
is minimal.
Then,
(\mathcal{A}[U],I)
is strictly balanced.
Furthermore, we have
\rho_{\mathcal{A}[U],I}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{A},I}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{F}}>0
and hence
U\neq I
.
Note that

(12.2)

\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\psi}=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}[U],\psi}^{\sim}}\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\varphi}.

We exploit the strict balancedness of
(\mathcal{A}[U],I)
to bound
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}[U],\psi}
and the induction hypothesis to bound
\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\varphi}
for all
\varphi\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}[U],\psi}^{\sim}
.

In detail, we argue as follows.
Due to Lemma
12.1
, we have

\Phi_{\mathcal{A}[U],\psi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{(1-\rho_{\mathcal{A}[U],I}/\rho_{\mathcal{F}})(\lvert U\rvert-\lvert I\rvert)}\mathclose{\}}=n^{\varepsilon^{3}}.

Furthermore, for all
\varphi\in\Phi_{\mathcal{A}[U],\psi}^{\sim}
, by induction hypothesis, we obtain

\Phi_{\mathcal{A},\varphi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert U\rvert)}.

Combining this with (
12.2
) yields

\Phi_{\mathcal{A}[U],\psi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot n^{\varepsilon^{3}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert U\rvert)}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}(\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}}\rvert-\lvert I\rvert)},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.7
.

\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\geq 1/(k-1)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is not a matching, there exist edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
with
e_{1}\cap e_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the
k
-graph with vertex-set
e_{1}\cup e_{2}
and edge-set
\mathopen{\{}e_{1},e_{2}\mathclose{\}}
.
Since
(\mathcal{F},e_{1})
is strictly balanced, we have

\rho_{\mathcal{F}}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{A},e_{1}}\geq\frac{1}{k-1},

which completes the proof
∎

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
\ell\leq 4
and suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
is a sequence of
k
-balanced
k
-graphs with
k
-density at least
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
and at most
m
vertices each that forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk.
Let
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
and suppose that there exists
e\in\mathcal{S}
with
\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}>\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Then
\Phi_{\mathcal{S}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{1/7}}
.

Proof.

Based on Lemma
12.3
, we distinguish two cases: The first case, where
\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
and the second case where
(\mathcal{S},\emptyset)
is strictly balanced.

First, suppose that
\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
.
From all
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\emptyset)\subseteq(\mathcal{S},\emptyset)
choose
(\mathcal{B},\emptyset)
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}
is maximal and subject to this, that
\lvert V_{\mathcal{B}}\rvert
is minimal.
Then,
(\mathcal{B},\emptyset)
is strictly balanced and we have
\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}\geq\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Furthermore, we have

(12.3)

\Phi_{\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi_{\mathcal{B}}^{\sim}}\Phi_{\mathcal{S},\varphi}.

For all
\varphi\in\Phi_{\mathcal{B}}^{\sim}
, due to Lemma
12.2
, we may apply Lemma
12.6
to obtain
\Phi_{\mathcal{S},\varphi}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
Furthermore, due to Lemma
12.1
, we have

\Phi_{\mathcal{B}}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{(1-\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}/\rho_{\mathcal{F}})\lvert V_{\mathcal{B}}\rvert}\mathclose{\}}=n^{\varepsilon^{3}}.

Returning to (
12.3
), due to Lemma
12.7
, this yields

\Phi_{\mathcal{S},\psi}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}n^{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot n^{\varepsilon^{3}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{1/7}},

and hence completes our analysis of the first case.

We proceed with the second case.
Hence, assume that
(\mathcal{S},\emptyset)
is strictly balanced and that
\rho_{\mathcal{S},\emptyset}=\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\rho_{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}<\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Then

n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}=n^{(1-\rho_{\mathcal{S},\emptyset}/\rho_{\mathcal{F}})\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert}\geq 1.

Thus, Lemma
12.1
entails

\Phi_{\mathcal{S}}\leq n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{3}}=n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k-(\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1)/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}.

If there exists
e\in\mathcal{S}
with
\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}>\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
, then since
\ell\leq 4
and
\lvert V_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\rvert\leq m
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
we have

\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{1/8}<\rho_{\mathcal{S},e}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k},

so we then obtain

\Phi_{\mathcal{S}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot n^{\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k-(\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{1/8})(\lvert V_{\mathcal{S}}\rvert-k)/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}<n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{1/7}},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
\ell\leq 4
and suppose that
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
is a sequence of copies of
\mathcal{F}
that forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk.
Let
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{A}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
.
If
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\geq 3
, that is if
\mathcal{F}
is strictly
k
-balanced, then
\Phi_{\mathcal{S}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{1/7}}
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
12.5
, this follows from Lemma
12.8
.
∎

13.
Bounding the number of copies of
\mathcal{F}

We assume the setup described in Section
11
and, similarly as in Section
5
, we define
\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)
to be the
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert
-graph with vertex set
\mathcal{H}(0)
whose edges are the edge sets of copies of
\mathcal{F}
that are subgraphs of
\mathcal{H}(0)
.
We now begin to analyze the
\mathcal{F}
-removal process formally again given by Algorithm
5.1
.
Again, if the process fails to execute step
i+1
and instead terminates, that is if
\mathcal{H}^{*}(i)=\emptyset
, then, for
j\geq i+1
, we set
\mathcal{H}^{*}(j):=\mathcal{H}^{*}(i)
.
For
i\geq 1
, we define
\mathcal{H}(i)
,
H^{*}(i)
,
H(i)
and the filtration
\mathfrak{F}(0),\mathfrak{F}(1),\ldots
as in Section
5
.
We again define the stopping time

\tau_{\emptyset}:=\min\mathopen{\{}i\geq 0\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}\mathcal{H}^{*}(i)=\emptyset\mathclose{\}}.

To prove Theorem
1.5
, in Section
14
, we show that the following theorem holds.

Theorem 13.1
.

If
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\geq 3
, then
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}H(\tau_{\emptyset})\leq n^{k-1/\rho-\varepsilon}\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-n^{1/4})
.

For our proof of Theorem
1.5
, in addition to the structural insights about configurations consisting of copies that we may encounter in
\mathcal{H}(0)
, we crucially rely on an upper bound for the number of copies of
\mathcal{F}
present in
\mathcal{H}(i)
for
i\geq 0
, which is the focus of this section.
First, note that initially, we may bound the number of copies as follows.
Let
\vartheta:=k!\,H(0)/n^{k}
.

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
i\geq 0
and
e\in\mathcal{H}
.
Then,
d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)\leq n^{m-k+\varepsilon^{7/2}}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}}
.

Proof.

By our assumptions on
\mathcal{H}(0)
, we have
n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{4}}\leq\vartheta\leq n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+2\varepsilon^{4}}
.
Hence, arguing similarly as in the proof of Lemma
12.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(e)\leq\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{\psi\colon f\hookrightarrow e}\Phi_{\mathcal{F},\psi}\leq\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\cdot n^{\varepsilon^{4}}\cdot\max\mathopen{\{}1,n^{m-k}n^{(-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+2\varepsilon^{4})(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\mathclose{\}}

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\cdot n^{\varepsilon^{4}}\cdot n^{m-k}n^{(-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{4})(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\cdot n^{3\varepsilon^{4}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\leq n^{m-k+\varepsilon^{7/2}}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}.

Furthermore, again using
\vartheta\leq n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+2\varepsilon^{4}}
, we obtain

n^{m-k+\varepsilon^{7/2}}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{7/2}}\cdot n^{2\varepsilon^{4}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\leq n^{\varepsilon^{3}},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.3
.

H^{*}(0)\leq n^{m+\varepsilon^{7/2}}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
13.2
, we obtain

H^{*}(0)=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}(0)}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)\leq\frac{\vartheta n^{k}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}\cdot n^{m-k+\varepsilon^{7/2}}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}\leq n^{m+\varepsilon^{7/2}}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert},

which completes the proof.
∎

13.1.
Heuristics

With the same justification as in Section
6
, we again assume that typically, for all
i\geq 0
, the edge set of
\mathcal{H}
behaves essentially as if it was obtained by including every
k
-set
e\subseteq V_{\mathcal{H}}
independently at random with probability

\hat{p}(i):=\vartheta-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\,i}{n^{k}}.

We may guess deterministic upper bounds for these numbers of copies that we expect to typically hold as follows by considering the expected one-step changes of these numbers.
Lemma
12.9
in particular shows that for almost all distinct edges
e,f\in\mathcal{H}(0)
, there exists at most one copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{H}(0)
of
\mathcal{F}
with
e,f\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Thus, for
i\geq 0
, for the one-step change
\Delta H^{*}
, we estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta H^{*}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{H}^{*}(i+1)\mathclose{]}\approx-\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}\frac{\big\lparen\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(f)\big\rparen-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert+1}{H^{*}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)^{2}}{H^{*}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1.

Using convexity and
H=\hat{p}n^{k}/k!
, this leads us to expect

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta H^{*}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\big\lparen\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)\big\rparen^{2}}{H\cdot H^{*}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1=-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}H^{*}}{H}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}k!\,H^{*}}{n^{k}\hat{p}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1.

Motivated by this, we aim to choose our deterministic upper bounds
\hat{h}^{*}(0),\hat{h}^{*}(1),\ldots
for the random variables
H^{*}(0),H^{*}(1),\ldots
such that, with some room to spare for estimation errors, they approximately satisfy

\Delta\hat{h}^{*}\geq-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}k!\,\hat{h}^{*}}{n^{k}\hat{p}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1.

By Lemma
13.3
, initially, that is for
i=0
, there are at most
n^{m+\varepsilon^{3}}\hat{p}^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}
copies of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{H}
.
With this initial condition, guided by the above intuition, for
i\geq 0
, we set

\hat{h}^{*}(i):=n^{m+\varepsilon^{3}}\hat{p}^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3}}+\frac{(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)n^{k}\hat{p}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\varepsilon^{3})k!}.

Observe that this expression is the sum of two parts where the second part is negligible up to step
i
where
\hat{p}\approx n^{-(m-k+\varepsilon^{3})/(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\varepsilon^{3})}
and where then, the first part becomes negligible.
For our argumentation, we focus our attention on the evolution of the process up to step
i^{\star}
, where

i^{\star}:=\frac{(\vartheta-n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{2}})n^{k}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}.

Note that following the above heuristic, for all
i\geq 0
and
e\in\mathcal{H}
, up to constant factors, we would expect approximately
n^{m-k}\hat{p}(i)^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}
copies of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{H}
that contain
e
, which suggests that the process should terminate around the step
i
where
\hat{p}\approx n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Since
i^{\star}
lies beyond this step, an analysis up to step
i^{\star}
should suffice.

13.2.
Formal setup

Formally, we argue similarly as in Sections
8.3
and
9.2
and phrase our statement about the boundedness of
H
from above for
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
in terms of the stopping time

\tau^{\star}:=\min\mathopen{\{}i\geq 0\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}H^{*}\geq\hat{h}^{*}\mathclose{\}}.

Our goal is to show that typically,
i^{\star}<\tau^{\star}
.
To this end, for a similar argumentation as in the aforementioned sections, for
i\geq 0
, define the critical interval

I(i):=[(1-\varepsilon^{4})\hat{h}^{*},\hat{h}^{*}].

For
i\geq 0
, let

Y(i):=H^{*}-\hat{h}^{*}.

For
i_{0}\geq 0
, define the stopping time

\tau_{i_{0}}:=\min\mathopen{\{}i\geq i_{0}\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}H^{*}\notin I\mathclose{\}}

and for
i\geq i_{0}
, let

Z_{i_{0}}(i):=Y(i_{0}\vee(i\wedge\tau_{i_{0}}\wedge i^{\star})).

Let

\sigma:=\min\mathopen{\{}j\geq 0\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}H^{*}\geq(1-\varepsilon^{4})\hat{h}^{*}\text{ for all }j\leq i<\tau^{\star}\wedge i^{\star}\mathclose{\}}\leq\tau^{\star}\wedge i^{\star}.

With this setup, similarly as in Sections
8.3
and
9.2
, it in fact suffices to consider the evolution of
Z_{\sigma}(\sigma),Z_{\sigma}(\sigma+1),\ldots
.

Observation 13.4
.

\mathopen{\{}\tau^{\star}\leq i^{\star}\mathclose{\}}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}Z_{\sigma}(i^{\star})>0\mathclose{\}}
.

We use Azuma’s inequality below to show that the probability of the event on the right in Observation
13.4
is sufficiently small.

Lemma 13.5

(Azuma’s inequality)
.

Suppose that
X(0),X(1),\ldots
is a supermartingale with
\lvert X(i+1)-X(i)\rvert\leq a_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
.
Then, for all
i\geq 0
and
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}X(i)-X(0)\geq t\mathclose{]}\leq\exp\bigg\lparen-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sum_{0\leq j\leq i-1}a_{j}^{2}}\bigg\rparen.

Before we turn to verifying that the conditions for an application of Azuma’s inequality in Sections
13.3
and
13.4
and applying the inequality in Section
13.5
, similarly as in Section
7
however now for the sparse setting that we consider since Section
11
, we gather some useful facts concerning key quantities defined up to this point.

Lemma 13.6
.

Let
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
.
Then,
n^{1-k-\varepsilon^{2}}\leq n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{2}}\leq\hat{p}\leq n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{3}}

Proof.

We have
n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{2}}=\hat{p}(i^{\star})\leq\hat{p}\leq\hat{p}(0)=\vartheta\leq n^{-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{3}}
.
With Lemma
12.7
, this completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.7
.

Let
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
.
Then,
\hat{p}(i+1)\geq(1-n^{-1/2})\hat{p}
.

Proof.

Lemma
13.6
implies

\hat{p}(i+1)=\bigg\lparen 1-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}{n^{k}\hat{p}}\bigg\rparen\hat{p}\geq\bigg\lparen 1-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}{n^{1-\varepsilon^{2}}}\bigg\rparen\hat{p}\geq(1-n^{-1/2})\hat{p},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.8
.

Let
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
and
\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i\leq\tau_{\emptyset}\mathclose{\}}
.
Then,
n^{1/2}\leq n^{k}\hat{p}/k!\leq H=_{\mathcal{X}}n^{k}\hat{p}/k!
.

Proof.

Indeed, we have
H\geq\vartheta n^{k}/k!-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert i=_{\mathcal{X}}H
and
\vartheta n^{k}/k!-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert i=n^{k}\hat{p}/k!
, so Lemma
13.6
completes the proof.
∎

13.3.
Trend

Here, essentially following the argumentation in Section
13.1
, we prove that for all
i_{0}\geq 0
, the expected one-step changes of the process
Z_{i_{0}}(i_{0}),Z_{i_{0}}(i_{0}+1),\ldots
are non-positive.
We bound the one-step changes of
\hat{h}^{*}
in Lemma
13.10
, then we turn to the non-deterministic one-step changes of
H^{*}
.
Crucially, to see that for
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
, the expected one-step changes of
H^{*}
are at most those of
\hat{h}^{*}
, which justifies our choice of
\hat{h}^{*}
, we employ Lemma
12.9
in the proof of Lemma
13.11
.

Observation 13.9
.

Extend
\hat{p}
and
\hat{h}^{*}
to continuous trajectories defined on the whole interval
[0,i^{\star}+1]
using the same expressions as above.
Then, for
x\in[0,i^{\star}+1]
,

\begin{gathered}(\hat{h}^{*})^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})k!\,\hat{h}^{*}}{n^{k}\hat{p}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1,\\
(\hat{h}^{*})^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\varepsilon^{3})(k!)^{2}n^{m+\varepsilon^{3}}\hat{p}^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3}}}{(n^{k}\hat{p})^{2}}.\end{gathered}

Lemma 13.10
.

Let
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
and
\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i\leq\tau_{\emptyset}\mathclose{\}}
.
Then,

\Delta\hat{h}^{*}\geq_{\mathcal{X}}-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})\hat{h}^{*}}{H}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\frac{n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Taylor’s theorem.

In detail, we argue as follows.
Together with Observation
13.9
, Lemma
7.10
yields

\Delta\hat{h}^{*}=-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\,(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})\hat{h}^{*}}{n^{k}\hat{p}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1\pm\max_{x\in[i,i+1]}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{4}(k!)^{2}n^{m+\varepsilon^{3}}\hat{p}(x)^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3}}}{(n^{k}\hat{p}(x))^{2}}.

We investigate the first term and the maximum separately.
Lemma
13.8
yields

\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!\,(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})\hat{h}^{*}}{n^{k}\hat{p}}=_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})\hat{h}^{*}}{H}.

Furthermore, using Lemma
13.7
and Lemma
13.8
, we obtain

\displaystyle\max_{x\in[i,i+1]}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{4}(k!)^{2}n^{m+\varepsilon^{3}}\hat{p}(x)^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3}}}{(n^{k}\hat{p}(x))^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in[i,i+1]}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{4}(k!)^{2}\hat{h}^{*}(x)}{(n^{k}\hat{p}(x))^{2}}\leq\frac{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{4}(k!)^{2}\hat{h}^{*}}{(n^{k}\hat{p})^{2}}

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{4}\hat{h}^{*}}{H^{2}}\leq\frac{n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}}{H},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.11
.

Let
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
.
Let
\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i<\tau_{i}\mathclose{\}}
.
Then,

\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta H^{*}\mathclose{]}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}H^{*}}{H}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1+\frac{n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}_{+}}{H}.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{F}_{2}
denote a collection of
k
-graphs
\mathcal{G}
with
V_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}1,\ldots,2m\mathclose{\}}
such that for all copies
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
of
\mathcal{F}
with
2\leq\lvert\mathcal{F}_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}_{2}\rvert\leq\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1
, the collection
\mathscr{F}_{2}
contains a copy of
\mathcal{F}_{1}+\mathcal{F}_{2}
and that only contains copies of such
k
-graphs.
We have

(13.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta H^{*}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{H^{*}}\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}\Big\lparen 1+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}(d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)-1)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e,f\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\\
e\neq f\end{subarray}}(d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(ef)-1)\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=-\bigg\lparen\frac{1}{H^{*}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)^{2}\bigg\rparen+\bigg\lparen\frac{1}{H^{*}}\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e,f\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\colon\\
e\neq f\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}\setminus\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\mathclose{\}}\colon\\
e,f\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}1\bigg\rparen+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1

\displaystyle\leq-\bigg\lparen\frac{1}{H^{*}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)^{2}\bigg\rparen+\bigg\lparen\frac{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}}{H^{*}}\sum_{\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{F}_{2}}\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}\bigg\rparen+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1.

We investigate the first two terms separately.

For the first term, using convexity, we obtain

(13.2)

\frac{1}{H^{*}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{HH^{*}}\Big\lparen\sum_{e\in\mathcal{H}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)\Big\rparen^{2}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}H^{*}}{H}.

Let us now consider the second term.
If
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert=2
, then
\mathscr{F}_{2}=\emptyset
and otherwise, for all
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{F}_{2}
,
Lemma
12.9
together with Lemma
13.6
and Lemma
13.8
entails

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{1/7}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon^{1/6}}(n^{1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}}\hat{p})^{2(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}

\displaystyle\leq n^{-\varepsilon^{1/5}}\cdot n^{k}\hat{p}\cdot n^{2(m-k)}\hat{p}^{2(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\leq\frac{n^{-\varepsilon^{1/4}}(\hat{h}^{*})^{2}}{n^{k}\hat{p}}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{n^{-\varepsilon^{1/3}}(\hat{h}^{*})^{2}}{H}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{n^{-\varepsilon^{1/2}}H^{*}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}.

Thus,

(13.3)

\frac{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}}{H^{*}}\sum_{\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{F}_{2}}\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}.

Combining (
13.2
) and (
13.3
) with (
13.1
) yields the desired upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta H^{*}\mathclose{]}
.
∎

Lemma 13.12
.

Let
0\leq i_{0}\leq i
.
Then,
\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta Z_{i_{0}}\mathclose{]}\leq 0
.

Proof.

Suppose that
i<i^{\star}
and let
\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i<\tau_{i_{0}}\mathclose{\}}
.
We have
\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta Z_{i_{0}}\mathclose{]}=_{\mathcal{X}^{\mathsf{c}}}0
and
\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta Z_{i_{0}}\mathclose{]}=_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta Y\mathclose{]}
, so it suffices to obtain
\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta Y\mathclose{]}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}0
.
Combining Lemma
13.10
with Lemma
13.11
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathopen{[}\Delta Y\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathcal{X}}-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}{H}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert H^{*}-(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})\hat{h}^{*})+\frac{2n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathcal{X}}-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}{H}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert(1-\varepsilon^{4})\hat{h}^{*}-(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\varepsilon^{3})\hat{h}^{*})+\frac{2n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\hat{h}^{*}}{H}+\frac{2n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}\leq 0,

which completes the proof.
∎

13.4.
Boundedness

For our application of Azuma’s inequality, it suffices to obtain suitable bounds for the absolute one-step changes of the processes
Y(0),Y(1),\ldots
and
Z_{i_{0}}(i_{0}),Z_{i_{0}}(i_{0}+1),\ldots
.
Furthermore, crude upper bounds that we obtain as an immediate consequence of the previously gained insights concerning the distribution of the copies of
\mathcal{F}
within
\mathcal{H}(0)
suffice.

Lemma 13.13
.

Let
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
.
Then,
\lvert\Delta Y\rvert\leq n^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
13.2
, Lemma
13.10
, Lemma
13.6
, Lemma
13.8
and the second inequality in Lemma
13.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert\Delta Y\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\Delta H^{*}\rvert+\lvert\Delta\hat{h}^{*}\rvert\leq\Big\lparen\sum_{e\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(i+1)}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)\Big\rparen-\Delta\hat{h}^{*}\leq\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert n^{\varepsilon^{3}}+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert+\frac{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}\hat{h}^{*}}{H}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert n^{\varepsilon^{3}}+2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert^{2}k!\,n^{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot n^{m-k}\vartheta^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}\leq n^{\varepsilon}.

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.14
.

Let
0\leq i_{0}\leq i\leq i^{\star}
.
Then,
\lvert\Delta Z_{i_{0}}\rvert\leq n^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Lemma
13.13
.
∎

13.5.
Supermartingale argument

Lemma
13.15
is the final ingredient that we use for our application of Azuma’s inequality in the proof of Lemma
13.16
where we show that the probabilities of the events on the right in Observation
13.4
are indeed small.

Lemma 13.15
.

Z_{\sigma}(\sigma)\leq-\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

Lemma
13.3
implies
\tau^{\star}\geq 1
and
H^{*}(0)<(1-\varepsilon^{4})\hat{h}^{*}(0)
, so we have
\sigma\geq 1
.
Thus, by definition of
\sigma
, for
i:=\sigma-1
, we have
H^{*}\leq(1-\varepsilon^{4})\hat{h}^{*}
and thus

Z_{i}=H^{*}-\hat{h}^{*}\leq-\varepsilon^{4}\hat{h}^{*}.

With Lemma
13.13
and Lemma
13.6
, this then yields

Z_{\sigma}(\sigma)\leq Z_{i}+\Delta Y\leq-\varepsilon^{4}\hat{h}^{*}+n^{\varepsilon}\leq-\varepsilon^{4}\hat{h}^{*}+n^{-2\varepsilon}n^{k}\hat{p}\leq-\varepsilon^{4}\hat{h}^{*}+n^{-\varepsilon}\hat{h}^{*}\leq-\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}.

Since
\Delta\hat{h}^{*}\leq 0
, this completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.16
.

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\tau^{\star}\leq i^{\star}\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-n^{1/3})
.

Proof.

Considering Observation
13.4
, it suffices to show that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{\sigma}(i^{\star})>0\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-n^{1/3}).

Due to Lemma
13.15
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{\sigma}(i^{\star})>0\mathclose{]}\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{\sigma}(i^{\star})-Z_{\sigma}(\sigma)>\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}\mathclose{]}\leq\sum_{0\leq i\leq i^{\star}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{i}(i^{\star})-Z_{i}>\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}\mathclose{]}.

Thus it suffices to show that for
0\leq i\leq i^{\star}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{i}(i^{\star})-Z_{i}>\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}\mathclose{]}\leq\exp(-n^{1/2}).

We show that this bound is a consequence of Azuma’s inequality.

Let us turn to the details.
Lemma
13.11
shows that
Z_{i}(i),Z_{i}(i+1),\ldots
is a supermartingale, while Lemma
13.14
provides the bound
\lvert\Delta Z_{i}(j)\rvert\leq n^{\varepsilon}
for all
j\geq i
.
Hence, we may apply Lemma
13.5
to obtain

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{i}(i^{\star})-Z_{i}>\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}\mathclose{]}\leq\exp\bigg\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{10}(\hat{h}^{*})^{2}}{2(i^{\star}-i)n^{2\varepsilon}}\bigg\rparen.

Since

i^{\star}-i\leq\frac{\vartheta n^{k}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}-i=\frac{n^{k}\hat{p}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!},

with Lemma
13.6
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}Z_{i}(i^{\star})-Z_{i}>\varepsilon^{5}\hat{h}^{*}\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{11}(\hat{h}^{*})^{2}}{n^{k+2\varepsilon}\hat{p}}\bigg\rparen\leq\exp\lparen-\varepsilon^{11}n^{k-3\varepsilon}\hat{p}\cdot n^{2(m-k)}\hat{p}^{2(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\exp\lparen-\varepsilon^{11}n^{k-3\varepsilon-2\varepsilon^{2}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1)}\hat{p}\rparen\leq\exp(-n^{1/2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

14.
The isolation argument

In this section, we show that
H(\tau_{\emptyset})\geq n^{k-1/\rho-\varepsilon}
with high probability if
\mathcal{F}
is strictly
k
-balanced.
For this section, in addition to the setup described in Section
12
, assume that
\mathcal{F}
is strictly
k
-balanced, so in particular that
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\geq 3
, and that
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{F}
-populated.
Overall, our approach is inspired by
[
6
, Proof of Theorem 6.1]
; however, whenever
\mathcal{F}
is not a triangle, copies of
\mathcal{F}
can form substructures that may prevent a direct translation of the argument.
For our more general setting, we rely on the insights gained in Section
12
to control these substructures in our analysis.

14.1.
Overview

Instead of choosing the edge sets of copies
\mathcal{F}_{0}(i)
with
i\geq 1
uniformly at random in Algorithm
5.1
, we may assume that during the initialization, a linear order
\preccurlyeq
on
\mathcal{H}^{*}
is chosen uniformly at random and that for all
i\geq 1
, the edge set
\mathcal{F}_{0}(i)
is the minimum of
\mathcal{H}^{*}(i-1)
.
Clearly, this yields the same random process.

Our argument that typically, sufficiently many edges of
\mathcal{H}(0)
remain when Algorithm
5.1
terminates may be summarized as follows.
We crucially rely on identifying edges of
\mathcal{H}(0)
that for some
i\geq 0
become isolated vertices of
\mathcal{H}^{*}
and hence remain at the end of the process.
We say that
almost-isolation
occurs at a copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)
if for some edge
e\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
at some step, the copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is the only remaining copy that contains
e
and we say that
isolation
occurs at
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
if additionally at a later step, a copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\neq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
with
e\notin\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset
is selected for removal hence causing
e
to become an isolated vertex in
\mathcal{H}^{*}
.

Initially, that is at step
i=0
, for every edge
e\in\mathcal{H}
, there exist at least two copies of
\mathcal{F}
that have
e
as one of their edges.
If at step
i=i^{\star}
we do not already have sufficiently many edges of
\mathcal{H}
that are isolated vertices of
\mathcal{H}^{*}
, then since by Lemma
13.16
we may assume that there is essentially not more than one copy of
\mathcal{F}
for every
\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert
edges that remain, we are in a situation where most of the remaining copies form a matching within
\mathcal{H}^{*}
.
Thus, almost-isolation must have occurred many times.

If it is the removal of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
during step
i
that causes almost-isolation at a copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, then before this removal, for all edges
e\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, there was a copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\neq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
with
e\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
and hence as a consequence of Lemma
12.9
, it only rarely happens that the removal of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
destroys all copies
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\neq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
that previously shared an edge with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Thus, in almost all cases where almost-isolation occurs, it is possible that isolation occurs.
Furthermore, it turns out that the probability that this happens is not too small.

We ensure that the copies at which we look for almost-isolation are spaced out as this allows us to assume that at these copies, almost-isolation turns into isolation independently of the development at the other copies.

14.2.
Formal setup

Formally, our setup is as follows.
For
\ell\geq 1
, a hypergraph
\mathcal{A}
and
e\in\mathcal{A}
, inductively define
\mathcal{N}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{A}}(e)
as follows.
Let
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(e):=\mathopen{\{}f\in\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}e\cap f\neq\emptyset\mathclose{\}}
denote the set of edges of
\mathcal{A}
that intersect with
e
and for
\ell\geq 2
, let

\mathcal{N}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{A}}(e):=\bigcup_{f\in N^{\ell-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(e)}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(f).

For
\ell\geq 1
, let
N^{\ell}_{\mathcal{A}}(e):=\lvert\mathcal{N}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{A}}(e)\rvert
.
During the random removal process, we additionally construct random subsets
\emptyset=:\mathcal{R}(0)\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{R}(i^{\star})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)
where we collect copies of
\mathcal{F}
at which almost-isolation occurs.
We inductively define
\mathcal{R}(i)
with
1\leq i\leq i^{\star}
as described by the following procedure.

Algorithm 14.1

Construction of
\mathcal{R}(i)
.

1

\mathcal{R}(i)\leftarrow\mathcal{R}(i-1)

2

consider an arbitrary ordering
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
of
\mathcal{H}^{*}(i)

3

for

\ell^{\prime}\leftarrow 1

to

\ell

do

4

if

i=\min\mathopen{\{}j\geq 0\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(j)}(e)=1\text{ for some }e\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell^{\prime}}\mathclose{\}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{F}_{\ell^{\prime}})\cap\mathcal{R}(i)=\emptyset

then

5

\mathcal{R}(i)\leftarrow\mathcal{R}(i)\cup\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}_{\ell^{\prime}}\mathclose{\}}

6

end if

7

end for

To exclude the copies at which almost-isolation occurs without the option that isolation occurs, we define subsets
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i)\subseteq\mathcal{R}(i)
as follows.
For
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(i^{\star})
, let

i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}:=\min\mathopen{\{}i\geq 0\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(i)\mathclose{\}}

be the step where
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is added as an element for the eventually generated
\mathcal{R}(i^{\star})
and for
i\geq 0
, let

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i):=\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(i)\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\neq\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\mathclose{\}}\mathclose{\}}

be the elements
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(i)
where at step
i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
, the copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
shared at least one edge with another copy of
\mathcal{F}
.
Finally, we define events that entail almost-isolation becoming isolation.
For
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i)
, fix an arbitrary
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\setminus\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\mathclose{\}}
and let

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}:=\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{G}\text{ for all }\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})\mathclose{\}}.

14.3.
Proof of Theorem
13.1

Since every almost-isolation that turns into isolation causes an edge of
\mathcal{H}(0)
to become an isolated vertex of
\mathcal{H}^{*}
for some
i\geq 0
and hence an edge that remains at the end of the removal process, we obtain the following statement.

Observation 14.1
.

H(\tau_{\emptyset})\geq\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star})}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}
.

We organize the formal presentation of the arguments outlined above in two lemmas.
At the end of the section, using the above observation together with these two lemmas, we prove Theorem
13.1
.

Define the event

\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\mathopen{\{}\lvert\mathopen{\{}e\in\mathcal{H}(i^{\star})\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(i^{\star})}(e)=0\mathclose{\}}\rvert<\varepsilon H(i^{\star})\mathclose{\}}

that occurs if and only if the number of isolated vertices of
\mathcal{H}^{*}(i^{\star})
is only a small fraction of all present vertices.

Lemma 14.2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau^{\star}\mathclose{\}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{0}
.
Then,
\lvert\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star})\rvert\geq_{\mathcal{X}}n^{k-1/\rho-4\varepsilon^{2}}
.

Proof.

Let
i:=i^{\star}
and consider the set

\mathcal{I}^{*}:=\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\mathopen{\{}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\mathclose{\}}\mathclose{\}}

of edge sets of copies of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{H}
that are isolated in the sense that they do not share an edge with another copy of
\mathcal{F}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}(0)
is
\mathcal{F}
-populated, by construction of
\mathcal{R}
, for every
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}
, either
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
itself is an element of
\mathcal{R}
or there exists some
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{R}
that prevented the inclusion of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{R}
.
Hence, there exists a function
\pi\colon\mathcal{I}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
that for every
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}^{*}
chooses a witness
\pi(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
with
\pi(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
or equivalently
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\pi(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}))
.
If
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and hence
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
.
Thus, Lemma
13.2
entails
\lvert\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\rvert\leq N^{4}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\leq n^{\varepsilon^{2}}
and so we have

(14.1)

\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert\leq\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\lvert\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\rvert\leq\lvert\mathcal{R}\rvert n^{\varepsilon^{2}}.

First, we obtain a suitable lower bound for
\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert
which, by the above inequality, yields a lower bound for
\lvert\mathcal{R}\rvert
, then we show that
\lvert\mathcal{R}\rvert
is essentially as large as
\lvert\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\rvert
.

Let us proceed with the first step.
Using Lemma
13.8
, we have

(14.2)

\displaystyle H^{*}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathcal{X}}\hat{h}^{*}\leq\bigg\lparen\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\varepsilon^{2}}+n^{m-k+2\varepsilon^{3}}\hat{p}^{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\varepsilon^{3}}\bigg\rparen\frac{n^{k}\hat{p}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{3/2}+n^{2\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{2}(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-1-\varepsilon^{3})+\varepsilon^{3}/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}})\frac{n^{k}\hat{p}}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\frac{H}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}.

From this, we obtain

(14.3)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathopen{\{}e\in\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\colonDelimiter}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)=0\mathclose{\}}\rvert+\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(e)}\leq_{\mathcal{X}}\varepsilon H+\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert+\bigg\lparen\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\bigg\rparen\lvert\mathcal{H}^{*}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert

\displaystyle=\varepsilon H+\bigg\lparen\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\bigg\rparen H^{*}+\frac{1}{2}\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert\leq_{\mathcal{X}}\varepsilon H+\bigg\lparen\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\bigg\rparen(1+\varepsilon)\frac{H}{\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}+\frac{1}{2}\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert

\displaystyle=H-\frac{1+\varepsilon-4\varepsilon\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}H+\frac{1}{2}\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert\leq H-\frac{1}{4\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}H+\frac{1}{2}\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert.

With Lemma
13.8
, this implies

(14.4)

\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}\rvert\geq_{\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}H\geq\frac{n^{k}\hat{p}}{2\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert k!}\geq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-2\varepsilon^{2}}.

Combining this with (
14.1
), we conclude that
\lvert\mathcal{R}\rvert\geq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-3\varepsilon^{2}}
, which completes the first step.

Consider a copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.
Let
e_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
There exists a copy
\mathcal{F}_{1}\neq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
with
\mathcal{F}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}-1)
such that
e_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{1}
.
Furthermore, there exists an edge
e_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{F}_{1}
and a copy
\mathcal{F}_{2}\neq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
with
\mathcal{F}_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}-1)
such that
e_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{2}
.
By choice of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
, both copies
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
have an edge that is contained in
\mathcal{F}_{0}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})
.
Hence, if
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{2}
does not form a self-avoiding cyclic walk, then, using
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
to denote the copy of
\mathcal{F}
with edge set
\mathcal{F}_{0}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})
, the sequence
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
forms a self-avoiding cyclic walk.
Thus, for every copy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, there exist copies of
\mathcal{F}
whose edge sets are elements of
\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)
and that together with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
form a self-avoiding cyclic walk of length
3
or
4
.

Let
\mathscr{F}_{4}
denote a collection of
k
-graphs
\mathcal{G}
with
V_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}1,\ldots,4m\mathclose{\}}
that for every self-avoiding walk
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
of copies of
\mathcal{F}
with
3\leq\ell\leq 4
contains a copy of
\mathcal{F}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
and that only contains copies of such
k
-graphs.
Then, we have
\lvert\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\rvert\geq\lvert\mathcal{R}\rvert-\sum_{\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{F}_{4}}4\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}
, so it suffices to show that
\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-4\varepsilon^{2}}
for all
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{F}_{4}
.
This is a consequence of Lemma
12.9
.
∎

Lemma 14.3
.

Suppose that
X
is a binomial random variable with parameters
n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-4\varepsilon^{2}}
and
n^{-\varepsilon^{2}}
and let
Y:=(n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-4\varepsilon^{2}}-\lvert\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star})\rvert)\vee 0
.
Let

Z:=Y+\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star})}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}.

Then,
Z
stochastically dominates
X
.

Proof.

First, observe that by Lemma
13.2
, whenever
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star})
, for
i:=0
, we have

(14.5)

N^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})\leq\sum_{f\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}d_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}(f)\leq n^{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Consider distinct
\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)
.
By construction of
\mathcal{R}(i^{\star})
, whenever
\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(i^{\star})
, then, for all
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(i_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
, we have

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}(0)}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset.

Thus, for all distinct
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star}_{+})
and all
z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell-1}\in\mathopen{\{}0,1\mathclose{\}}
, from (
14.5
), we obtain

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}}=1\mathrel{|}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{\ell^{\prime}}}}=z_{\ell^{\prime}}\text{ for all }1\leq\ell^{\prime}<\ell\mathclose{]}=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}\mathclose{]}\geq n^{-\varepsilon^{2}},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 14.4

(Chernoff’s inequality)
.

Suppose
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are independent Bernoulli random variables and let
X:=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}X_{i}
.
Then, for all
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}X\neq(1\pm\delta)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{[}X\mathclose{]}\mathclose{]}\leq 2\exp\bigg\lparen-\frac{\delta^{2}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{[}X\mathclose{]}}{3}\bigg\rparen.

Proof of Theorem
13.1
.

Define the events

\mathcal{B}:=\mathopen{\{}H(\tau_{\emptyset})\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon}\mathclose{\}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{X}:=\mathopen{\{}i^{\star}<\tau^{\star}\mathclose{\}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{0}.

We need to show that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{[}\mathcal{B}\mathclose{]}
is sufficiently small.
Choose
X
,
Y
and
Z
as in Lemma
14.3
.
Lemma
14.2
entails
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathopen{\{}Y=0\mathclose{\}}
and hence
\mathopen{\{}Y\neq 0\mathclose{\}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{\mathsf{c}}
.
Thus, from Observation
14.1
and Lemma
14.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{\Big\{}\sum_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i^{\star})}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon}\mathclose{\Big\}}\cap\mathcal{B}\subseteq\lparen\mathopen{\{}Z\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon}\mathclose{\}}\cup\mathopen{\{}Y\neq 0\mathclose{\}}\rparen\cap\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathopen{\{}Z\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon}\mathclose{\}}\cup(\mathcal{X}^{\mathsf{c}}\cap\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathopen{\{}Z\leq n^{k-1/\rho_{\mathcal{F}}-\varepsilon}\mathclose{\}}\cup\mathopen{\{}\tau^{\star}\leq i^{\star}\mathclose{\}}\cup(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\mathsf{c}}\cap\mathcal{B}).

By Lemma
13.8
, we have
